GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-1

Question:

During 2022 did the Company make any additions to Plant Held for Future Use?  If so, include a description of why each item was purchased and how the purchase is related to a project that was approved in the Company's 2022 Integrated Resource Planning case. For each addition, provide a list showing the property location, date acquired, date expected to be in utility service and FERC Account 105 balance as of December 31, 2022. 

Response:

The Company did not make any additions to Plant Held for Future Use during 2022.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-11.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-11

Question:

Provide the actuarial reports that correspond with the Prepaid Pension Asset on Section 3, page 1 of 6.  
Please also reconcile the Prepaid Pension Asset with those actuarial reports, and show the reconciling details.
Is there an ADIT balance that relates to the Prepaid Pension Asset?  If so, please identify the related ADIT balance and show in detail the income tax rates that were used to derive it.

Response:

The prepaid pension asset total on Section 3, page 1 of 6, represents the 13-month average balance of both Georgia Power Company’s (GPC) prepaid pension asset as well as the prepaid pension amount associated with Southern Nuclear Company (SNC).

In preparing the response for this data request, the Company discovered the SNC prepaid pension asset balances in the 2022 ASR filing were inadvertently misstated because it included nonqualified pension expenses. The impact of the correction to the retail rate base is an increase of $2.7 million on a 13-month average basis, which results in a decrease in 2022 ASR sharing amount of $224 thousand.

The revised monthly balances for SNC and total prepaid pension asset balances are provided below.

The monthly balances for GPC and SNC are provided below:

The amounts presented in the above table for SNC represents SNC’s total prepaid pension asset; therefore, amounts will not tie to the SNC prepaid pension asset allocated to GPC as shown in response to part “a” above. The SNC prepaid pension asset allocated to GPC is based on the actual pension expense billed from SNC to GPC based on SNC labor charges, as well as contributions GPC has made to SNC’s pension fund, and not based on a unit or plant allocation. Since the prepaid pension asset allocated from SNC to GPC is based on the cumulative contributions and actual monthly pension expense billed over time spanning several decades, it would be extremely time-consuming and difficult to reconcile the balance on GPC’s books to the total SNC prepaid pension asset on the actuarial reports. However, to illustrate the allocation process, GPC has provided an allocation of the SNC net pension expense added to the prepaid pension asset for December 2022 in STF-ASR-9-11 Attachment B.

b. Yes, please see STF-ASR-9-11 Attachment C.

adjustments, as shown in Section 3, Page 1.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-12.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-12

Question:

ADIT. Refer to the ASR, Section 3, page 1 of 3, Accumulated Deferred Income Taxes.
Please provide the ADIT amounts by FERC account by month from December 2021 through December 2022 by temporary difference.
For each of the ADIT amounts identified in response to part a. of this question, please identify where the related temporary difference is added to or subtracted from rate base.  If the related temporary difference is not added to or subtracted from rate base, please explain why it is not and provide all authorization for this treatment relied on by the Company.

Response:

Please see STF-ASR-9-12 Attachment. In preparing the response for this data request, the Company discovered the ADIT FERC account 283 was inadvertently misstated by $233 thousand on a 13-month average basis. The impact of the correction is an increase in retail rate base by $233 thousand, which results in a decrease in 2022 ASR sharing amount of $20 thousand. The attachment reflects the corrected ADIT amount.

Please see the column “Reference” in STF-ASR-9-12 Attachment for the location of the related temporary difference in rate base.


===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-13.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-13

Question:

Refer to the 2022 ASR, Section 2, page 1 of 2.  Please show in detail how each of these remaining plant net book value amounts and regulatory asset amounts in the table below were accumulated and how they are being amortized:

      

Show the related 2022 journal entries by month that affected each of these balances.

Response:

The amounts presented in Section 2, page 1 of 2 represent 13-month average balances ending December 2022. Please see Section 5, Schedule 7 in the 2022 ASR filing for the monthly balances for all items except ARO Regulatory Asset, for which, the monthly balances are provided in Section 5, Schedule 15.

The Environmental CWIP regulatory asset was recorded in December 2013 and has been fully amortized over a period ending December 2022, as ordered by the Commission in Docket No. 36989.  The monthly amortization entry was a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to amortization expense (FERC account 407) of $0.61 million in January through December 2022.

The Unamortized NBV of Retired Units regulatory asset is comprised of the net book values for Plant Branch Units 2 – 4 and Common, Plant Mitchell Unit 3 and Common, Plant Hammond Units 1-4, Plant Estatoah Unit 1, Plant Langdale Units 5-6, Plant Riverview Units 1-2, Plant McIntosh Unit 1, Plant Wansley Units 1, 2, and 5A CT, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1 CT as well as unusable materials and supplies inventory associated with Plant Wansley.

The Plant Branch Unit 2 NBV regulatory asset was recorded in September 2013 and has been fully amortized over a period ending December 2022 as ordered by the Commission in Docket No. 34218. The Plant Branch Units 3, 4, and Common NBV regulatory asset was recorded in April 2015 and is being amortized over a period ending December 2023 for Unit 3, and December 2024 for Unit 4 and Common as ordered by the Commission in Docket Nos. 34218 and 36498. The monthly amortization entry was a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to amortization expense (FERC account 407) of $1.26 million in January through December 2022.
  
The Plant Mitchell Unit 3 and Common NBV regulatory asset, including portable equipment, of $12.71 million was recorded in August 2016 and was amortized through 2019 over a period equal to the remaining useful life approved by the Commission in Docket No. 36989.  Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42516, the remaining net book value as of December 31, 2019 has been fully amortized over a 3-year period ending December 2022.  The monthly amortization entry was a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to amortization expense (FERC account 407) of $0.13 million in January through December 2022.

Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42310, the remaining NBV of Plant Hammond Units 1-4 of $501.58 million was reclassified as a regulatory asset in July 2019 and was amortized through December 2019 over a period equal to the remaining useful lives of the units as approved by the Commission in Docket No. 36989.  Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42516, the remaining net book value of Plant Hammond Units 1-4 will continue to be amortized over a period equal to the remaining useful lives approved by the Commission in Docket No. 36989.  The monthly amortization entry was a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to amortization expense (FERC 407) of $3.13 million in January through December 2022.

Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42310, the remaining NBV of Plant Estatoah Unit 1, Plant Langdale Units 5-6, and Plant Riverdale Units 1-2 of $0.95 million was reclassified as a regulatory asset in July 2019 and was amortized through December 2019 over a period equal to the remaining useful lives as approved by the Commission in Docket No. 36989.  Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42516, the remaining net book values as of December 31, 2019 has been fully amortized over a 3-year period ending December 2022. The monthly amortization entry was a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to amortization expense (FERC 407) of $25 thousand in January through December 2022.  

Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42310, the remaining NBV of Plant McIntosh Unit 1 of $38.50 million was reclassified as a regulatory asset in July 2019 and was amortized through December 2019 over a period equal to the remaining useful life as approved by the Commission in Docket No. 36989. Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42516, the remaining net book value as of December 31, 2019 has been fully amortized over a 3-year period ending December 2022. The monthly amortization entry was a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to amortization expense (FERC 407) of $1.04 million in January through December 2022.

Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 44160, the remaining NBV of Plant Wansley Units 1, 2, and 5A CT, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1 CT was reclassified as a regulatory asset in August 2022 and were amortized through December 2022 over a period equal to the remaining useful life as approved by the Commission in Docket No. 42516. As approved by the Commission in Docket No. 44280, the remaining net book values of Plant Wansley Units 1 and 2 will be amortized over a period equal to the remaining useful life approved by the Commission in Docket No. 42516 and the remaining net book values of Plant Wansley Unit 5A CT and Plant Boulevard Unit 1 CT will be amortized over a 3-year period beginning January 1, 2023.

Also pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 44160, the unusable materials and supplies inventory associated with Plant Wansley was re-classified as a regulatory asset in September 2022 with amortization to be determined in the next base rate case as approved by the Commission in Docket No. 44280.

Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42516, the Company will record costs to the regulatory asset TOU – FD Revenue Erosion for any revenue erosion in 2020 through 2022 from the conversion of certain retail customers to the Time of Use – Food and Drink tariff (“TOU-FD”) to be amortized and recovered from all TOU-FD customers in the following year. The monthly entry to defer revenue erosion was a debit to the regulatory asset and credit to retail revenue (FERC 442) in January through December 2022, which totaled $1.18 million for 2022. Based on the 2022 compliance filing, the Company recorded a monthly amortization entry of a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to retail revenue (FERC 442) of $0.06 million in January through December 2022.

Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42516, certain expenses associated with the implementation of software and cloud computing projects are being deferred to the Software & Cloud Computing regulatory asset and the projected balance as of December 2022 will be amortized over a 5-year period beginning January 2023 as approved by the Commission in Docket No. 44280.

The OPRB Retiree Drug Subsidy regulatory asset was recorded in January 2011 and has been fully amortized over a twelve-year period ending December 2022, as ordered by the Commission in Docket No. 31958.  The monthly amortization entry was a credit to the regulatory asset balance and a debit to amortization expense (FERC account 407) of $0.35 million in January through December 2022.  

Please see the Company’s response to STF-ASR-9-16 for the details related to the deferred nuclear outage costs.  

The FERC 182 ARO Regulatory Asset balance is not amortized.  Under ARO Accounting, this balance is increased by the deferral of the depreciation of the FERC 101 ARO Plant asset and accretion of the FERC 230 ARO liability.  The balance is decreased by the cash expenditures related to ARO settlements.  The balance also includes the retirement of ARO net book value of decertified plants.


===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-14.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-14

Question:

Refer to the Summary of Ratemaking Principles Used in Computing Retail Return on Equity, Section 1 (pages 5 and 6), Section 5, Schedule 7, of the 2022 ASR and the table below, which summarizes the Other Rate Base items that comprise the line item “Unamortized NBV of Retired Units” in the amount of $645.399 million in Section 2, page 1 of the 2022 ASR.  

     

Item number 34 on the Summary of Ratemaking Principles Used in Computing Retail Return on Equity states:

Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42310, the remaining net book values of Plant McIntosh Unit 1, Plant Estatoah Unit 1, Plant Langdale Units 5-6, and Plant Riverview Units 1-2 have been reclassified as a regulatory asset and have been fully amortized through 2022.  Pursuant to the Commission’s Order in Docket No. 42310, the remaining net book values of Plant Hammons Unit 1-4 have been reclassified as a regulatory asset and are being amortized over a period equal to the remaining useful life approved by the Commission in Docket No. 36989 as approved by the Commission in Docket No. 42516.

As noted in the table and passage above, other than Plant Hammond Units 1-4, except as noted in parts “b” and “c” below, the plants listed in the table do not agree with what the remaining retired plants listed in Item 34.  Please explain and reconcile these discrepancies.  Identify, quantify and explain each reconciling item.

Please confirm that the amounts listed for (1) Plant Wansley Units 1-2, (2) Plant Wansley Unit 5a CT, and (3) Plant Boulevard Unit 1 CT relate to Item Number 40 from the Summary of Ratemaking Principles Used in Computing Retail Return on Equity.  If not confirmed, explain fully why not.

Please confirm that the amount listed for Unusable Inventory relates to Item Number 41 from the Summary of Ratemaking Principles Used in Computing Retail Return on Equity.  If not confirmed, explain fully why not.

Response:

In addition to the units listed in Item 34 in the Summary of Ratemaking Principles Used in Computing Retail Return on equity and the items in parts ‘b’ and ‘c’, the $645.399 million is also comprised of the Unamortized NBV of Retired Units noted in Items 28, 29, and 31, which are applicable to Plant Branch Unit 2, Plant Branch Units 3, 4 and Common, and Plant Mitchell Unit 3, respectively. Please note, “Hydro Plants” in the table referenced in the question refers to Plant Estatoah Unit 1, Plant Langdale Units 5-6, and Plant Riverdale Units 1-2 listed in Item 34. Please also see the Company’s response to STF-ASR-9-13 for additional details.

Yes, Item Number 40 in the Summary of Ratemaking Principles Used in Computing Retail Return on Equity is related to Plant Wansley Units 1, 2, and 5A CT, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1 CT.

Yes, Item Number 41 in the Summary of Ratemaking Principles Used in Computing Retail Return on Equity is related to Unusable Materials and Supplies Inventory associated with Plant Wansley.

STF-ASR-9-18

Question:

Distribution Asset Management and Vegetation Management.

Show by month of 2022 the Company's budgeted amounts by account for distribution asset management.
Show by month of 2022 the Company's actual cost by account for distribution asset management.
Show by month of 2022 the Company's budgeted amounts by account for distribution vegetation management.
Show by month for 2022 the Company's actual cost by account for distribution vegetation management.
Identify and document all Company decisions in 2022 to defer or delay distribution vegetation management.
Show by month of 2022 the Company's budgeted amounts by account for transmission vegetation and right of way management.
Show by month for 2022 the Company's actual cost by account for transmission vegetation and right of way management.
Identify and document all Company decisions in 2022 to defer or delay transmission vegetation and right of way management.
Did the Company have a written plan for vegetation management in 2022?

If not, explain fully why not.  
If so, please identify and provide such plans.

Response:

Please see STF-ASR-9-18 Attachment.

Please see STF-ASR-9-18 Attachment.

Please see STF-ASR-9-18 Attachment.

Please see STF-ASR-9-18 Attachment.

There were no decisions to defer or delay distribution vegetation management in 2022.
 
Please see STF-ASR-9-18 Attachment.
Please see STF-ASR-9-18 Attachment.

There were no decisions to defer or delay transmission vegetation management in 2022. 

The Company’s vegetation management plan is as follows:
Transmission Herbicide – Work plan consists of one-third system miles to be sprayed each year.

Re-clearing – Work plan is based on approximately one-sixth of system miles.

Yard Tree Removal - Scheduled based on the forester’s assessment of the condition of lines within their territories, with respect to planted incompatible species on the Right-of-Way. Lines that are most heavily impacted, and where conductor clearance is approaching critical are prioritized highest. In highly urbanized areas, yard tree removal should be performed approximately every ten years.

Danger Tree – At least one patrol on all transmission lines. 

Transmission Side Tree Trimming - Established work plan determined by due date of next scheduled activity for each line and the criticality of the line; critical lines are high voltage bulk lines. Cycle is 8-12 years based on vegetation type and right of way conditions. 

Transmission Urban Tree Trimming - Established work plan determined by due date of next scheduled activity for each line. Cycle is an average of three years based on vegetation type and right of way conditions.

Distribution Herbicide – Work plan is based on a two-year cycle where approximately half of the system miles are sprayed each year.

Distribution Tree Trimming – Cycle typically ranges from 24-60 months, with an average of approximately three and a half years. Cycles are based on vegetation type and right of way conditions.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-19.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-19

Question:

Refer to Section 5, Schedule 6.
Show by month of 2022 the Company's budgeted amounts by account for generating unit outages.
Show by month for 2022 the Company's actual cost by account for generating unit outages.
Identify and document all Company decisions in 2022 to defer or significantly change the scope of generating unit outages.

Response:

Please see STF-ASR-9-19 Attachment.
Please see STF-ASR-9-19 Attachment.
The Company did not defer any outages in 2022.  Overall, the Company was over budget for fossil, hydro, and nuclear generating unit outages in 2022. This increase in cost of outages and outage scope were partially offset with underruns in non-outage costs. Individual FERC accounts may vary due to granularity of actuals versus budgets, as well as unknowns related to outage cost and scope at the time budgets are developed.
All generation outage scopes are budgeted based on expected duration.  The ability to inspect equipment and make the necessary repairs may not be fully known until a generating unit is brought offline.  Once a generating unit is brought offline, the Company can commence with discovery work, which facilitates decisions regarding what repairs will be made during such outage.  As a result of information gathered during this process, the cost and level of repairs may vary from the budget and are appropriately managed.  There is no specific discrete documentation regarding these efforts, as it is part of the Company’s normal operation.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-20.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-20

Question:

Refer to Section 5, Schedule 6.

Please explain fully and in detail the reason(s) for the overruns which resulted in actual Distribution related vegetation and asset management as well as incidental maintenance and storm costs of $444.037 million being $83.991 million higher than the budgeted amount of $360.046 million.

Please explain fully and in detail the reason(s) for the overruns which resulted in actual Administrative & General expense attributable to shared service billings and labor costs of $520.079 million being $89.204 million higher than the budgeted amount of $430.874 million. 

Response:

The variance in Distribution is primarily related to overruns in Distribution vegetation and asset management of $35.6 million due to increased maintenance and repair activity as well as increased tree trimming to eliminate backlog, incidental maintenance of $34.2 million driven by increased Distribution investments,, and base labor costs related to storms of $6.4 million. Please see the Company’s response to STF-ASR-9-18 for additional details.  

The variance in Administrative & General expenses of $89.2 million is primarily due to overruns in shared service billings (primarily SCS and Southern Linc) $53 million, GPC Labor $18 million, and Contract Labor $18 million. Overruns are partially attributed to a budget misclassification with dollars being budgeted in other non-A&G FERC accounts, but actual charges recorded to A&G FERC accounts.

GEORGIA POWER COMPANY

Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-23

Question:

Did the Company record AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities during 2022?
If so, when in 2022 did the Company start recording AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities? 
Identify, quantify, and explain the impacts on 2022 results, by account, for recording AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities.
If not, explain fully why not.  
Explain fully the Company's policy for recording AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities and provide the related accounting policies.
Please cite by date and docket number, any Commission Orders which authorized the recording of AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities.
Was there any cumulative result of any accounting change to record AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities?
If so, identify when that occurred and identify the impact on the Company's 2022 ASR results.

Response:

The Company does not record AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities.

Please see the Company’s response to part ‘a’ above. 

FERC guidance dictates that suspended payments, contract retentions, unbilled expenditures, unpaid property taxes, and other accruals that will not be paid within a reasonable billing cycle should not be included in the AFUDC allowance base.  Conversely, construction expenses paid during a normal business cycle are considered expenditures eligible for inclusion in the AFUDC base. 

Please see the Company's response to part ‘a’ above. While it would not be inappropriate to accrue AFUDC on unrecorded liabilities for invoices received that will be paid within a reasonable billing cycle, the Company has not included these costs in its AFUDC base. Please see STF-ASR-9-23 Attachment for related accounting policies. 

Please see the Company’s response to part ‘a’ above.

Please see the Company’s response to part ‘a’ above.

STF-ASR-9-25

Question:

Refer to Section 5, Schedule 12, Major Tax Changes. 
a.Did the Company have any bonus tax depreciation in 2022 (for projects with binding contracts before September 27, 2017 and placed into service after September 28, 2017)?
If so, please identify the amounts and show how they affected the ADIT balances shown for 2022 rate base.  If not, explain fully why not.

b.As of 1/1/2022 or 12/31/2022 did the Company have any uncertain tax positions?
2.If so, identify, quantify, and explain each uncertain tax position as of each date.

 c.If applicable, identify and provide a copy of all journal entries made in 2022 (and the related journal entry support) related to recording the impacts of bonus tax depreciation.

Response:

The Company did not have any bonus tax depreciation claimed on the assets in rate base that were placed into service in tax year 2022.  As a result of the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act, the Company no longer qualifies for bonus depreciation for regulated utility assets. 

The Company recorded an uncertain tax position in September 2021 of $254,017 related to the tax benefit of the fair market appraisal of the Plant Kraft land donation to the Georgia Ports Authority.  This uncertain tax position was recorded to non-utility operations and excluded from retail revenue requirements in 2021 and was reversed in 2022 after the 2021 tax returns were accepted.

Not applicable.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-30.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY

Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-30

Question:

Additional sum amounts for DSM.  Refer to Section 6, Workpaper 8 - Demand Side Management Program.  Please show in detail how the additional sum amount of $12.277 million listed on Workpaper 8 was derived.  Show detailed calculations. 

Response:

kWh and expenses source: “Certified Demand-Side Management Programs, Fourth Quarter 2022 Programs Status Report,” Docket 193314, filed by the Company on February 15, 2023

Avoided cost source:  Determined by the Program Plans, settled by the Company and PSC Staff on July 29, 2019

Net-to-Gross ratio source: Determined by program evaluation reports, filed by the Company on August 14, 2018
Additional Sum calculation source: “Final Order in Response to Docket 42311, Georgia Power Company’s Application for the Certification of Amended Demand Side Management Plan,” decided July 29, 2019

Program Expenses = sum of expenses by program + 50/50 split of DSM Cross Cutting / Training between Residential & Commercial

Example:
Residential:
HEIP - $4,225,566.48
HEEAP - $6,225,527.02
Thermostat DR - $1,355,234.24
Res Behavioral - $1,312,168.87
Specialty Lighting - $3,425,209.85
Refrigerator Recycling - $1,456,748.34

Res Programs Total - $18,000,454.80 + $1,085,950.83 ($2,171,901.65 ÷ 2) = $19,086,405.63


Net Avoided Cost = avoided costs by measure, by program    X   kWh    X    Net-to-Gross
Example: 
HEIP - $0.35 ($0. 348829481894782) avoided cost X 14,066,672 kWh X 92.8% NTG = $4,553,575.27

Net Benefit = Avoided costs – Program Costs including cross cutting 
Example: 
HEIP - $4,553,575.27 – ($4,225,566.48 + (1,085,950.83 1/6)) = $147,016.99


Additional Sum = Net Benefit   X   8.5%
Example (Residential Sector): 
$14,784,223.96 X 8.5 % = $1,256,659.04

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-31.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-31

Question:

Stock-Based Compensation.   

Please explain fully and in detail why the Company did not include an adjustment in its 2022 ASR filing to remove the costs associated with stock-based compensation from operating expenses and the associated ADITs, as it has done over the past several years in accordance with the Commission's Order in Docket No. 36989.

Please provide a breakout, in a manner similar to Section 6, Workpaper 11 from past ASR filings (i.e., O&M functions and ADIT account 190), of the components of stock-based compensation included in the 2022 ASR.

Please confirm that the stock-based compensation amounts provided in part “b” reflect 100% of the stock-based compensation attributable to GPC, Southern Company Services and Southern Nuclear Services.  If not confirmed, explain fully why not.

Response:

The 2019 base rate case settlement approved by the Commission in Docket No. 42516 provided for the inclusion of stock-based compensation within retail cost of service under the 3-year alternate rate plan. Specifically, paragraph 33 in the Commission’s Order adopting the Stipulation states, “…PIA Staff’s recommendation to remove the stock compensation plan from rate base is denied.”  The Commission also found that “the Proposed Agreement does not include this adjustment and the Commission finds that the Settlement Agreement as Modified is reasonable as a whole.”  Consequently, the revenue requirement approved in the settlement reflected full recovery of stock-based compensation expenses.

Please see STF-ASR-9-31 Attachment.

The stock-based compensation amounts provided in STF-ASR-9-31 Attachment in the Company’s response to part ‘b” above reflect 100% of the stock-based compensation attributable to GPC, Southern Company Services, and Southern Nuclear Company.

GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-32

Question:

Performance Pay Plan.  
Provide a copy of the Performance Pay Plan (PPP) that was in effect for 2022 and for 2023 including all changes and amendments to such plan.
Identify the amounts for PPP recorded directly by Georgia Power Company by account in 2022 and 2023 to date. 
Identify the amounts for PPP charged to Georgia Power Company broken out by each affiliate (SCS, SNOC, other affiliates), by account in 2022 and 2023 to date.
Show in detail the goals, targets and achieved levels of each key performance measure that related to a 2022 PPP award.  Include supporting documentation.

Response:

Please note, the Company is providing only 2022 information requested in this data request as the requested information for 2023 is not within the scope of the cost of service reflected in the 2022 ASR filing.

Please see STF-ASR-9-32 Attachment A.

Please see STF-ASR-9-32 Attachment B. Approximately $139 million of PPP costs were reflected in the Company’s 2022 cost of service. PPP amounts booked to the FERC account series 500s and 900s are the Company’s operating expenses and accounts related to fuel.  FERC account 184s are clearing accounts assumed to be 60% O&M and 40% capital, and FERC account 183 (PS&I) is assigned 100% to capital.  

Please see STF-ASR-9-32 Attachment C for PPP costs allocated to the Company by SCS and SNOC.  The Company does not get a direct allocation of PPP through any other affiliate.

Please see STF-ASR-9-32 Attachment D for PPP targets and PPP actual results for 2022.

STF-ASR-9-4

Question:

During 2022, did the Company record any costs related to Coal Combustion Residuals?
If so, explain fully and provide the amounts by account.
Please explain fully and in detail whether there was a change in the Company's Asset Retirement Obligations related to Coal Combustion Residuals during 2022.  If so, provide the related accounting entries made in 2022.
Also show by line item in the ASR “Average Rate Base” (Section 2 Page 1) and “Operating Income” (Section 2 Page 2) where the costs are reflected.

Response:

Please see the Company’s response to STF-ASR-9-3 for costs related to the closure of ash ponds and landfills.  

In addition, there were $0.4 million of capital costs and $9.4 million of operations and maintenance costs related to Coal Combustion Residuals at various plants. Please see STF-ASR-9-4 Attachment.

In 2022, the Company recorded a change in the asset retirement obligation account related to coal combustion residuals after completing an assessment of its plans to close the ash ponds at all of its generating plants in compliance with the CCR Rule and the related state rule. Cost estimates were revised to reflect further refined costs for closure plans and updates to the timing of future cash outlays. The journal entries to record the 2022 CCR ARO updates are as follows:

(To retire and reclassify ARC Asset (FERC 101) to regulatory asset for previously retired/converted generating plants due to end of life)

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-46.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-46

Question:

PPP in 2022 related to Vogtle Units 3 and 4. 
 
Identify how much PPP cost related to Vogtle Units 3 and 4 is included in the Company's retail jurisdictional rate base for 2022, by line item, on the Company's 2022 ASR filing Schedule 2, page 1 of 2.
Identify how much PPP cost related to Vogtle Units 3 and 4 was included in the Company's retail jurisdictional operating expenses for 2022, by line item, on the Company's 2022 ASR filing Schedule 2, page 2 of 2.
Identify and provide the subjective assessment related to Vogtle Units 3 and 4 that applied for 2022 PPP analysis.
How did the subjective assessment related to Vogtle Units 3 and 4 that was applied for 2022 PPP analysis take into account these items (1) project's schedule in 2022 versus originally planned schedule, and (2) project’s cost in 2022 versus originally planned project cost.
What would the amounts listed in responses to parts a. and b. of this request be without inclusion of PPP awards related to Vogtle Units 3 and 4?  If exact amounts are not available, provide the Company's best estimates and show in detail how such estimates were calculated.

Response:

The Company objects to this question.  In the 2014 ASR, the Georgia PSC Staff concluded that any decision to include or exclude impacts to the Company’s PPP expense of particular items is within the discretion of the Southern Company Board Compensation Committee (“Compensation Committee”).  This conclusion was adopted by the Commission in its Order on the 2014 Annual Retail Surveillance Report dated February 24, 2016 in Docket No. 36989 (“2014 ASR Order”).  This question, therefore, seeks information that is not relevant to the current proceeding in light of the 2014 ASR Order as the Commission has already concluded that no adjustment shall be made to the ASR on the basis of the Compensation Committee’s exercise of discretion as contemplated in the Southern Company Omnibus Incentive Compensation Plan.

The Staff subsequently reached the same conclusion in its report on the 2015, 2016, 2017, and 2018 ASR and this position was also once again adopted by the Commission.  Furthermore, nothing in the 2019 Rate Case Order or the Stipulation adopted by the Commission in that case or the 2022 Rate Case Order alters the discretion of the Compensation Committee or the recovery of PPP expenses in retail rates. 

Under a hypothetical scenario in which the Plant Vogtle Units 3 and 4 PPP goal is excluded and the remaining operational goals are re-weighted, the GPC PPP payout percentage would remain the same (172%), the SCS payout percentage would have decreased to 171% from 172%, and SNC’s PPP payout percentage would have decreased to 163% from 165%, resulting in an approximate $2.52 million of decreased PPP cost for GPC. Applying the same assumptions regarding the split between capital and O&M used in the response to STF-ASR-9-32, removing the Plant Vogtle Units 3 and 4 goal would result in a decrease of retail operating expenses of approximately $0.95 million and a decrease of retail rate base of approximately $1.57 million.

Please see STF-ASR-9-46 Attachment for the qualitative items which were considered by GPC executive management and approved by the Compensation and Management Succession Committee of the Southern Company Board of Directors in assigning a 175% assessment value for the Plant Vogtle Units 3 & 4 goal included in the Company’s 2022 Performance Pay Program.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-52.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-52

Question:

Prepaid pension asset.  Is any amount for Southern Company Services' net pension asset included in the Company's retail jurisdictional rate base for 2022, by line item, on the Company's 2022 ASR filing Schedule 2, page 1 of 2?  If not, explain fully why not.   If so, identify how much by FERC account.  If exact amounts are not available, provide the Company's best estimates and show in detail how such estimates were calculated.

Response:

No, the Company does not include any of Southern Company Services’ (SCS) net pension asset in retail rate base.  The capitalized portion of the pension costs, which are billed to the Company by SCS on a monthly basis, are included in rate base. The billed amounts are imported into the Company’s general ledger and distributed among the various FERC accounts included in the Company’s 2022 ASR filing Section 2, page 1 of 2.

The estimates of the pension cost billed to the Company are not easily determined.  This is because pension amounts billed by SCS to the Company, and subsequently distributed among the various FERC accounts in the Company’s general ledger, are calculated by the use of allocations and cost pools. Some of the allocations used in the process utilize dynamic allocation rates which use direct labor dollars as the allocator. Therefore, there is not a consistent allocation rate that can be applied to recalculate exact amounts billed to the Company because the direct labor billed to the Company changes each month. Also, during the initial SCS billing process, the pension dollars are accumulated in a cost pool and comingled with other benefits before being allocated to the Company’s direct labor charges.  Because the pension costs are not allocated as individual dollars, are comingled with other benefits cost, and are incorporated into direct labor charges, it would be extremely difficult to estimate the amount of pension costs billed to the Company.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-6 Attachment B.pdf =====
                                                                    STF-ASR-9-6 Attachment B


12.0 Obsolete Materials

Obsolete materials are items that have lost their intrinsic value and must be removed for
disposition. Items identified and verified as obsolete materials MUST be removed from stock
within 90 days of identification and adjusted from inventory on the company’s books.


Identification and Approval
Obsolete materials cannot be identified and approved by the same employee. Plant obsolete
material may be identified by the system owner (engineer). T & D obsolete material may be
identified by a staff engineer. Plant management and T & D management must have
documentation of appropriate review of obsolete material.


Items become obsolete in one of two ways:
1. Technology
Changes in technology render an item unusable.
• Parts support an asset that is no longer used.
• The part is no longer used when it has been replaced by a different part.


2. Shelf-Life Expirations
• Items lose quality over time.
• Items are deemed obsolete if they exceed established life standards.


A report of slow-moving material should be reviewed annually. It is the responsibility of Supply
Chain Management (Transmission, Distribution, Substation) and system owners (Plants) to
insure the evaluation every item on the report as soon as practicable. System upgrades and
changeouts should include a review of potential stranded material. Guidelines for plant
obsolescence review and material disposition are detailed in the Southern Company Generation
Playbook Chapter XIII.


For items stocked at the MDC, the MDC will notify region locations of pending obsolete
material items. The locations will be allowed four weeks to return any items to the MDC. After
the four week period, the region supervisor will work with the storekeeper to insure appropriate
disposition of obsolete items.

                                                                      STF-ASR-9-6 Attachment B


Inventory adjustments entered in Maximo must include “obsolete” as the adjustment reason. Any
other information necessary to explain the adjustment is also included in the “comments” field.
Documentation must be retained locally for one year for audit purposes. Contact the appropriate
financial analyst for appropriate obsolete materials project and task.


Fleet obsolete material adjustments must be approved by the System Administrator and the Fleet
Financial Analyst.
Surplus Material
Upon conclusion of a project, surplus standard approved materials should immediately be taken into
stock.

Materials which are non-standard, or which are known to be slow moving or obsolete, should not
be placed in stock. The Supply Chain Management Supervisor should contact the Investment
Recovery Manager (or designee) to determine the disposition of the materials.

Refer to 2022 ASR Section 2, page 1.

STF-ASR-9-65

Question:

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-66.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-66

Question:

For each temporary difference identified in response to the previous question, indicate whether the Company believes the related ADIT is protected or unprotected.  If the Company believes that the ADIT is protected, provide all citations to the IRC, Regulations, PLRs, and all other authoritative sources that address whether the ADIT is protected or unprotected.

Response:

ADIT accounts are either “protected” and subject to the Internal Revenue Code (IRC) normalization requirements, or “unprotected” and not subject to normalization requirements. Protected accounts are those that are the result of book-tax depreciation timing differences.
To be entitled to accelerated methods of depreciation for tax purposes, the Company must use a normalization method of accounting as required by IRC sections 168(f)(2) and 168(i)(9). Specifically, protected ADITs result from the excess of tax depreciation deductions allowable under IRC section 168 (Modified Accelerated Cost Recovery System and Accelerated Cost Recovery System), and depreciation deductions that would be allowable under IRC section 167(l).  Unprotected ADITs, which include all ADITs other than protected (regardless of whether they are property-related such as basis differences), are not addressed in the IRC nor the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act.  
As such, in the Company’s response to STF-ASR-9-65, the Company classified excess accelerated depreciation ADITs in FERC accounts 281 and 282 as protected and all other regulated temporary items as unprotected and not subject to IRS normalization requirements.
As previously stated, the Internal Revenue Code requires the Company to use normalization as a prerequisite to claim accelerated depreciation. If these tax benefits are not normalized in the ratemaking process, the utility loses the right to claim the benefits. In addition, section 13001(d)(1) of the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act states that a normalization method of accounting shall not be treated as being used with respect to any public utility property for purposes of section 167 or 168 of the Internal Revenue Code of 1986 if the taxpayer, in computing its cost of service for ratemaking purposes and reflecting operating results in its regulated books of account, reduces the excess tax reserve more rapidly or to a greater extent than such reserve would be reduced under the average rate assumption method.

Yes, please see STF-ASR-9-70 Attachment A.

The Tech Square Microgrid is located at 755 Williams St. NW, Atlanta, Ga 30308. This is the only microgrid currently owned by Georgia Power Company. The microgrid consists of 1 diesel engine (625 kW), 2 natural gas generators (400 kW total), 1 Bloom Energy Server/Fuel Cell (200 kW), and a battery energy storage system (200 kW/400kWh). The Tech Square Microgrid closed to plant in-service in October 2020.

Please see STF-ASR-9-70 Attachment B for additional details about the project.

No portion of the of the costs were funded by a specific participant/customer.

STF-ASR-9-71

Question:

Regarding the fire that occurred at the Bowen Unit 2 Phase 3 GSU on July 13, 2017, in the settlement of the 2017 ASR, the Company agreed that "any additional retail costs associated with repairing and/or replacing the equipment related to the transformer fire incurred in 2018 up to the deductible be excluded from retail cost of service and the insurance proceeds already received and any additional proceeds received be applied to costs incurred in 2018 and beyond as appropriate."
Please identify by amount and account, any costs incurred in 2022 related to the Bowen Unit 2 transformer fire that are included in the 2022 ASR retail rate base.
Please identify by amount and account, any costs incurred in 2022 related to the Bowen Unit 2 transformer fire that are included in the 2022 ASR retail cost of service.
Please identify the amount of any insurance proceeds received in 2022. State whether any costs related to the Bowen Unit 2 transformer fire that are included in the 2022 ASR have been offset by the amount of such insurance proceeds.  If not, explain fully why not.

Response:

The Company did not include any costs associated with the July 2017 Bowen Unit 2 Phase 3 GSU fire (GSU fire) in the 2022 ASR retail rate base.

The Company did not include any costs associated with the July 2017 GSU fire in the 2022 ASR retail cost of service.

The Company did not receive insurance proceeds in 2022 related to the GSU fire.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-74.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-74

Question:

Refer to 2022 ASR, Section 5, Schedule 13.  For the following items, please provide a detailed description, explain what is included within the description, and provide a spreadsheet which shows in detail how the 13-month average for each item was calculated.

Transmission Capital Maintenance / Asset Management
Transmission Grid Investment
Distribution Grid Investment
Distribution New Business
Distribution Capital Maintenance / Asset Management
Software Purchases and Upgrades 

Response:

Please see STF-ASR-9-74 Attachment for details on how the 13-month average was calculated for items ‘a’ through ‘f’.
Transmission Capital Maintenance/Asset Management includes capital costs related to the improvement and replacement of failed, obsolete, or deteriorating substation equipment and transmission line equipment.
Transmission Grid Investment includes capital costs to replace aging transmission system assets that have exceeded or will exceed their Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan.  This investment mitigates future reliability challenges associated with the transmission system.

Distribution Grid Investment includes capital costs to address the Company’s approximately 1,000 underperforming distribution feeders (feeders with SAIDI and SAIFI 5-year average performance in the third and fourth quartiles across the Georgia Power system) to improve reliability for the over 1 million customers served from those feeders.

Distribution New Business includes capital costs related to providing new service to customers of all revenue classes. The costs are net of any contributions in aid of construction received from customers.

Distribution Capital Maintenance/Asset Management includes capital costs related to the improvement and replacement of failed, obsolete, or deteriorating distribution equipment.

Software Purchases and Upgrades includes capital costs related to the procurement and enhancement of software, primarily driven by costs related to Enterprise Foundation Cloud Computing.

STF-ASR-9-76

Question:

During 2022, did GPC make payments to settle lawsuits?  If so, identify the amounts paid for each case and describe each case in detail.  Indicate whether the payment or payments were included in retail expenses and whether they were recorded above or below the line. If any such payments were included in retail expenses (above the line), please identify where such expenses are reflected in the 2022 ASR filing and explain and justify their inclusion in retail expenses.

Response:

Yes, please see STF-ASR-9-76 Attachment for payments made by the Company to settle lawsuits in 2022. This attachment is trade secret and has been redacted in its entirety for public disclosure purposes.  The Company excluded settlements of matters involving condemnation of private property for use as a utility easement as they do not constitute lawsuits filed against the Company.  
In general, compromise settlements of lawsuits are “above the line” expenses because such costs are normal operating expenses of the regulated business.  In 2012, FERC required the Company to classify expenses resulting from a compromise settlement of employment lawsuits “below the line.”

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-78.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-78

Question:

Identify all Nuclear Regulatory Commission fees paid in calendar years 2018 through 2022. In addition, break out each fee in detail by month and type (licensing, inspection, special projects, annual, etc.).  Explain how and where such fees are reflected in the 2022 ASR.

Response:

The following table contains the fees paid to the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) during 2018 through 2022.


Note: Dollars reflect GPC Ownership %

The following tables show the fees paid to the NRC by month during 2018, 2019, 2020, 2021 and 2022.


Note: Dollars reflect GPC Ownership %

The fees for Plants Hatch and Vogtle Units 1 and 2 are recorded to FERC account 524 and included in “Generation – Fuel & Variable O&M” expense in Section 2, Page 2 in the 2022 ASR. The fees for Plant Vogtle Units 3 and 4 are recorded to CWIP and excluded from retail cost of service. The following is an overview of the process by which the fees are set and the factors impacting the variability of such fees.

Each year, Congress approves the NRC Budget. Once the budget is approved, the NRC goes through a rulemaking process to establish fixed fees and hourly rates. Approximately 90% of the NRC’s budget is recovered by setting fees for their various activities and licensees.

NRC Fixed Fees:
The NRC calculates the annual fixed fee by dividing the recoverable budgeted costs equally among the licensed power reactors (currently 93 operating reactors).

NRC Variable Fees:  
The NRC uses an hourly rate to assess fees for specific services provided by the NRC
Variable fees fluctuate from year to year, and are calculated using the hourly rate and the number of hours required for NRC activities performed such as: 
Inspections 
License amendment reviews
Operator license training

STF-ASR-9-81

Question:

How and in what amounts were the Commission-ordered Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (TCJA) refunds made by the Company in 2022?  Explain in detail and show the amounts of the TCJA refunds by date and by rate class.

Response:

There were no TCJA refunds made by the Company in 2022. As ordered by the Commission, the Company provided the last remaining third of the TCJA refunds to customers in February 2020 based on their actual base billings for the period May 2019 through December 2019.

===== DKT 42516 STF-ASR-9 PD/STF-ASR-9-9.docx =====
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
Docket No. 42516
Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Annual Surveillance Report
Staff Data Request No. STF-ASR-9


STF-ASR-9-9

Question:

For each Plant, provide the Company's coal inventory policies and procedures and target amounts that applied for 2022.

Response:

The Company’s coal inventory target is set in accordance with the Commission’s Final Order issued in FCR-22 (Docket No. 33302). Actual inventories are not static, and it is common for actual inventory levels to fluctuate above and below targets. The purpose of inventory targets is to reasonably assure that Georgia Power can meet its obligation to provide electricity even during events of supply disruption, whether mine or rail-related, and during periods when coal burn exceeds projections. To this end, the Company purchases coal and plans for deliveries based on maintaining its inventory within the inventory targets and takes prudent actions to return plant inventories to target when they deviate. However, it is important to note that although the Company plans its procurement with a target of an overall average of 40-50 days of inventory across all plants over the course of a year, monthly targets for a particular plant may be set outside the range. Please see STF-ASR-9-9 Attachment A for coal inventory policies and procedures.  Also, please see STF-ASR-9-9 Attachment B for 2022 monthly targets.
Please note, the information in STF-ASR-9-9 Attachment A is trade secret and redaction of such information, due to the volume of such information, would require manual redaction and would be time consuming, overly burdensome, and would impede the Company’s timely response. Upon request, the Company will provide a redacted version.
